● Let's go! 🚀 I've turned on Guided Tutorial Mode. I'll be your mentor while
  you build our new robot (a Kraken swerve drivetrain with a turret shooter) one
  step at a time. If you ever just want me to write the code, say "just do it."

  About my notes: I'll keep two notes files as we go. TUTORIAL_PROGRESS.md
  tracks where you are in the tutorial. LEARNINGS.md tracks what you've learned
  and what was confusing. They help coaches see your progress and make the
  tutorial better. You can read them anytime or ask me what's in them. I just
  created both.

  Where we are: Phase 0, the very start. By the end of today's session you'll be
  driving our swerve robot around a simulated field with a real controller, and
  watching it move on our dashboard.

  ▎ Coming up:
  ▎ - Git branches (C05): your own copy of the code, so you can experiment 
  ▎ without breaking anyone else's work.
  ▎ - Simulation (C41): running the real robot code on your laptop, with physics
  ▎ standing in for the motors.
  ▎ - The 20 ms loop (C02): the robot doesn't run your code once. It runs it 50 
  ▎ times a second, forever.
  ▎ - The dashboard (C42): your window into what the robot is "thinking."
  ▎
  ▎ Ask me about any of these whenever you're curious.

  Step 1: You're already on the fresh-start branch in VS Code, so WPILib looks
  set up. ✅

     is.

     you change code on your branch?

  Step 3: Start the simulator 🎮

  Now you'll run the real robot code on your laptop, with a physics model
  playing the part of the motors.

  1. Plug in an Xbox-style controller.

  3. Click ChurroDashboard to open http://localhost:5800.

     connected. Then enable Teleop.

  Quick prediction before you drive: if you push the left stick forward, which
  way will the robot move on the Field card? Toward the top or toward the
  bottom?

  Step 4: Drive

  In the Sim Driver Station, enable Teleop and try these:

  - Left stick: moves the robot
  - Right stick: turns it
  - Hold A: faces 0°
  - Press X: locks the wheels in an X shape (good for not getting pushed)

  Step 5: Watch the Field card

  The Field card shows the field from the driver's point of view, so your 
  alliance wall is at the bottom. Try switching alliance in the Sim Driver
  Station and watch the field flip.

  This dashboard is how we'll check everything we build from now on: speeds, arm
  angles, whether the shooter is ready, and so on. You can show anything the
  robot knows on it, and you can change how any of it looks.

  Try this: spin the robot so it faces sideways, then push the left stick
  forward. Which way does it go: "up the field" or the way the robot's front is
  facing? Tell me what happens. It's a clue to how the drive code works.

● Nice! Now for your first code edit. ✍️

  Step 6: Show the robot's speed as a number

  New idea: logging (C13)

  The robot knows a lot: wheel speeds, angles, where it is. But we can't see any
  of it unless the code publishes it. That's what this line does:

  Logger.recordOutput("Some/Name", value);

  Think of it like posting to a group chat. The robot posts a value under a name
  50 times a second, and anyone listening (our dashboard, or AdvantageScope)
  can read it. If you want to see something, recordOutput it.

  Your mission: in Drive.java, inside periodic(), log the robot's speed in
  meters per second under the name Tutorial/Drive/SpeedMetersPerSec.

  Hint to get started: Drive.java already has a method that returns how fast the
  robot is moving. Can you find it? Look around line 283. What does it give
  back, and is that a single number?

  Take a look and tell me what you find. Or try writing the line yourself and
  I'll check it.

✻ Crunched for 15s · done Wednesday 2:31 AM

❯ public chassisspeeds get chasisspeeds9) {                                     

● You found it! 🎯 It's public ChassisSpeeds getChassisSpeeds().

  Here's the catch: a ChassisSpeeds isn't one number. It's a bundle of three:

  - vxMetersPerSecond: how fast the robot moves forward/back
  - vyMetersPerSecond: how fast it moves sideways (swerve can do that!)
  - omegaRadiansPerSecond: how fast it spins

  We want one number: how fast the robot is moving overall, in any direction.

  Puzzle: say the robot moves 3 m/s forward and 4 m/s sideways at the same time.
  How fast is it actually going? 🤔 (Hint: think of the triangle you'd draw.)

  Once you have the math, Java has a built-in helper for it: Math.hypot(a, b).
  Try writing the full line in periodic() and paste it here. I'll check it
  before you save.

✻ Cooked for 4s · done Wednesday 2:33 AM

❯ 5m/s                                                                          

● Exactly right! 🎉 It's 5 m/s, because 3² + 4² = 5². That's the Pythagorean
  theorem, and it's what Math.hypot(a, b) does for you.

  Now put it together. Here's the shape, with blanks for you to fill in:

  ChassisSpeeds speeds = ________;
  double speed = Math.hypot(speeds.________, speeds.________);
  Logger.recordOutput("Tutorial/Drive/SpeedMetersPerSec", speed);

  Where it goes: at the very end of periodic() in Drive.java, right after the
  line with gyroDisconnectedAlert.set(...) (around line 182), before the closing
  }.

  Fill in the blanks and paste it here, or type it straight into the file and
  save.

  Step 7: Put your speed on the dashboard 📊

  Your value travels like this:

  ▎ robot code → recordOutput → NetworkTables → dashboard

  On the dashboard side, everything AdvantageKit logs gets
  /AdvantageKit/RealOutputs/ added to the front of its name.

  Your file: src/main/deploy/dashboard/custom-dashboard.js

  The Field card already reads the robot's pose (where it is) and shows it as
  text (x 3.21 · y 4.05 · 90°). You'll copy that same pattern for speed. Look at
  these spots:

  - Line 4: POSE_TOPIC = "/AdvantageKit/RealOutputs/Odometry/Robot" names the
    value it reads.
  - Line 79: poseText.textContent = ... writes the text you see on the card.
  - Line 90: a list of topic names. When any of them changes, the card redraws.

  Guiding question: if you wanted the card to show speed too, what would you
  need to add at each of those three spots? Make a guess for each one, even a
  rough one, and I'll help you turn it into code.

● Good progress! You've done 2 of the 3 spots. 👍

  ✅ Spot 3 (line 91): you added your topic to the list, so the card will redraw
  when speed changes. Nice.

  🔧 Spot 1 (line 5): one bug. Your name is missing the prefix. Remember that
  AdvantageKit adds something to the front of every name it logs. Compare your
  line with POSE_TOPIC right above it: what's different?

  Small style tip: the other names all end in _TOPIC (POSE_TOPIC,
  ALLIANCE_TOPIC). Calling yours SPEED_TOPIC would match them. Matching the
  surrounding code makes a file easier to read.

  ⬜ Spot 2 (around line 80): not done yet. The card still only shows the pose
  text. Look at how pose gets read:

  const pose = decodePose2d(core.getTopic(POSE_TOPIC)?.value);

  Speed is just a plain number, so you don't need decodePose2d. Question: how
  would you read your speed value the same way, and then add it to the end of
  the poseText.textContent string?

  Fix the prefix, take a shot at spot 2, and ask me to check again.

❯ now?                                                                          

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Nice rename. SPEED_TOPIC matches the other names now. ✅

  Two things are still left:

  🔧 The prefix is still missing. Put the two lines side by side:

  const POSE_TOPIC  = "/AdvantageKit/RealOutputs/Odometry/Robot";
  const SPEED_TOPIC = "Tutorial/Drive/SpeedMetersPerSec";

  The pose name starts with /AdvantageKit/RealOutputs/ and yours doesn't. Add
  that to the front of yours. Without it, the dashboard looks for a topic that
  doesn't exist.

  ⬜ Spot 2 isn't done yet. Here's the shape. Put it in render(), right after
  the const pose = ... line:

  const speed = core.getTopic(SPEED_TOPIC)?.value;

  Then change the end of the poseText.textContent line so it adds your speed.
  For example, add this inside the backticks, after the °:

   · ${speed?.toFixed(2)} m/s

  toFixed(2) rounds the number to 2 decimal places, so you see 1.53 instead of
  1.5312876….

  Try both and ask me to check again!

  Phase 1: How that just worked 🔍

  You drove the robot, but how does pushing a stick make the wheels move? In
  this phase we follow that path through the code, so you can change it with
  confidence.

  ▎ Coming up:
  ▎ - Subsystems (C06): a piece of the robot that owns its own motors, like 
  ▎ "Drive."
  ▎ - Commands (C07): an action the robot does, like "drive with the joystick."
  ▎ - Default commands (C09): what a subsystem does when nobody asks it to do 
  ▎ anything else.
  ▎ - Triggers (C08): connecting a button press to a command.
  ▎
  ▎ Ask me about any of these whenever you're curious.

  Step 1: Follow the stick press

  The path looks like this:

  ▎ controller → RobotContainer → DriveCommands.joystickDrive → 
  ▎ Drive.runVelocity → each swerve module

  We'll start at the beginning. Open RobotContainer.java and look for where the
  controller's left stick gets used. Search for getLeftY.

  Question: what method is that code inside of, and what does the name of that
  method suggest it does?

❯ its in the method configure buttonbindings, maps the commands                 

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

● Exactly right. ✅ configureButtonBindings() connects controller inputs to
  commands. Look at the first thing inside it (lines 103–108):

  drive.setDefaultCommand(
      DriveCommands.joystickDrive(
          drive,
          () -> -controller.getLeftY(),
          () -> -controller.getLeftX(),
          () -> -controller.getRightX()));

  New idea: commands (C07)

  A command is an action the robot does: "drive with the joystick," "spin the
  intake," "lock the wheels in an X." DriveCommands.joystickDrive(...) doesn't
  drive anything when it runs. It builds a driving action and hands it over, and
  the robot runs that action every 20 ms.

  Here's an analogy. drive is a worker (that's a subsystem, which owns the drive
  motors). A command is a job you give that worker. A worker can only do one
  job at a time, so if a new job comes in, the old one stops.

  Prediction question: the method is called setDefaultCommand. Based on that
  name, when do you think the joystick-driving job runs? Always? Only when a
  button is held? Something else?

✻ Sautéed for 9s · done Wednesday 2:52 AM

❯ it stays ar 0 degrees                                                         

● Good observation. That's what you'd see! But why does it stay at 0°? Here's
  the trick:

  - While A is held: joystickDriveAtAngle is running, and it actively steers the
    robot to 0°.
  - When you let go: that job ends, and drive goes back to its default command,
    regular joystick driving. Nothing is holding the robot at 0° anymore. It
    just isn't turning, because your right stick is centered, which means "turn
    speed = 0."

  It's like a car: when you let go of the steering wheel, it keeps pointing the
  way it was pointing.

  Test it: hold A, then let go and push the right stick. Does it turn freely?
  (Now try pushing the right stick while holding A. What happens?)

  Tell me what you see, then we'll open DriveCommands.java.

● That's the idea. ✅ Anything between −0.1 and 0.1 counts as zero, so 0.05
  becomes 0. Without a deadband, the robot would slowly creep or spin on its own
  while the controller sits on the table, because the stick never reads exactly
  0. That's annoying in a match and dangerous in the pit.

  (A small detail: values outside the deadband get rescaled, so the output still
  goes smoothly from 0 to 1 instead of jumping from 0 straight to 0.1.)

  ---

  Next line: squaring (line 83)

  omega = Math.copySign(omega * omega, omega);

  This squares the stick value. copySign keeps the sign, so pushing left still
  turns left.

  Puzzle: push the stick halfway (0.5) and all the way (1.0). After squaring,
  what do the two values become? Then guess why drivers would want that. Think
  about lining up carefully for a shot vs. racing across the field.

● Perfect. ✅ Half-stick gives only a quarter of the speed, so small stick

  One more mystery: the minus signs

  Go back to RobotContainer.java, lines 106–108:

  () -> -controller.getLeftY(),
  () -> -controller.getLeftX(),
  () -> -controller.getRightX()));

  Every stick value gets a minus sign in front of it. Here are two facts:

  1. On an Xbox controller, pushing the stick forward (up) gives a negative Y
     value. (It's a leftover from old flight joysticks.)
  2. On the robot, forward is positive X and left is positive Y. Turning
     counter-clockwise is positive.

  Question: why do you think we need the minus sign on getLeftY()? What would
  happen when you push the stick forward if we removed it?

● Exactly. ✅ The minus sign flips the controller's "forward = negative" into
  the robot's "forward = positive." Without it, your driver would push forward
  and the robot would back into the wall. You now understand all three ways the
  stick gets cleaned up: deadband, squaring, and sign flips (C28). 🎉

  ---

  Last idea in this step: field-relative driving (C25)

  Look at DriveCommands.java, line 93:

  ChassisSpeeds.fromFieldRelativeSpeeds(speeds, drive.getRotation())

  There are two ways a robot could understand "push the stick forward":

  - Robot-relative: "drive the way the robot's front is pointing." Like an RC
    car: if the car faces you, forward on the stick drives it toward you.
    Confusing!
  - Field-relative: "drive up the field, away from my wall, no matter which way
    the robot faces." The code uses the gyro (drive.getRotation()) to do the
    math.

  Try it in the sim: spin the robot so it faces sideways (or backwards), then
  push the left stick forward. Which way does it go on the Field card: up the
  field, or the way the robot's front points? And based on line 93, which kind
  of driving does our robot use?

● Right. ✅ Our robot drives field-relative. Pushing forward always goes up the
  field, whichever way the robot faces. Drivers love it, because they never have
  to think "which way is the robot facing right now?"

  The rest of the path: drive.runVelocity(...) takes those field-relative
  speeds, and kinematics math (C25) turns them into a speed and an angle for
  each of the 4 wheels. Each module then spins its motors to match. That
  completes the path:

  ▎ stick → RobotContainer (sign flip) → joystickDrive (deadband, squaring, 
  ▎ field-relative) → Drive.runVelocity (kinematics) → 4 modules → wheels 🛞

  ---

  Step 3: Who runs all this? Robot.java

  New idea: the 20 ms loop (C02)

  Robot code doesn't run once from top to bottom like a normal program. It runs
  in a loop, 50 times a second, forever. Every 20 ms the robot reads its
  sensors, decides what to do, and sets its motors. It's like a flipbook: each
  page is one quick snapshot, and flipping fast makes it look like smooth
  motion.

  Open Robot.java and look at robotPeriodic() (line 105), especially line 119:

  CommandScheduler.getInstance().run();

  That one line is the heartbeat. Every 20 ms it checks buttons, starts and
  stops commands, runs the active ones, and calls every subsystem's periodic().
  That includes the Drive.periodic() where you logged speed!

  Check yourself: say someone wrote this inside periodic() to wait for a button
  press:

  while (!controller.a().getAsBoolean()) { }

  Why is that a really bad idea? (Hint: think about what happens to the other 49
  "pages" that second.)

● You're on the right track. ✅ Here's the full reason it's really bad, not just
  slow.

  The robot has one cashier handling everything: the drivetrain, the intake, the
  shooter, the logging. Every 20 ms, the cashier serves each subsystem quickly
  and moves on. A while loop that waits for a button is like one customer saying
  "hold on, let me find my wallet…" while the line stops completely:

  - The other subsystems' periodic() methods never run.
  - No new commands start, and the dashboard stops updating.
  - The motors are stuck doing whatever they were last told. If the robot was
    driving at full speed, it keeps going. 😬

  The fix is to check once each loop ("is A pressed? no? fine, check again next
  loop") and never wait. That's what triggers like controller.a().whileTrue(...)
  do for you.

  ---

  Step 4: Why could we drive without a robot? (IO layers, C12)

  This is one of the most important ideas in the whole tutorial, because every
  mechanism you build will use it.

  Look in src/main/java/frc/robot/subsystems/drive/. You'll see three related
  files:

  - ModuleIO.java
  - ModuleIOKraken.java
  - ModuleIOSim.java

  Guess from the names: what do you think each of these three files is for? And
  why do you think there are two versions (Kraken and Sim)?

● That's a great guess, and you got it without any hints. ✅ Here's the idea
  behind it.

  New idea: IO layers (C12)

  Think of a wall outlet. The outlet is a standard shape. You can plug in a
  lamp, a phone charger, or a toaster, and the outlet doesn't care which one it
  is, because they all fit the same plug.

  - ModuleIO is the outlet. It's an interface, a list of what any swerve module
    must be able to do: updateInputs (read sensors), setDriveVelocity,
    setTurnPosition, and so on. It has no motor code at all.
  - ModuleIOKraken is one plug: real Kraken and SPARK MAX motors.
  - ModuleIOSim is another plug: a physics model pretending to be motors.

  Now look at RobotContainer.java, lines 43–64:

  case REAL:  new ModuleIOKraken(0, "FrontLeft"), ...
  case SIM:   new ModuleIOSim(), ...

  That's the only place that picks which plug to use. Drive.java never knows the
  difference. It just calls io.setDriveVelocity(...). That's why you could
  drive on a laptop with no robot.

  Check yourself: say the team swaps the Kraken drive motors for a different
  brand next year. Which file(s) would need to change, and which would stay
  exactly the same: Drive.java, ModuleIO.java, ModuleIOKraken.java, or
  DriveCommands.java?

  too (autos, wheel-speed limits), so it's a real robot setting, not just a
  joystick setting.

  ---

  Step 6: Your first button binding 🔘

  Now you'll write a binding instead of just reading one.

  New idea: triggers (C08)

  A trigger connects a condition (like "B is pressed") to a command. The way you
  connect it changes when the command runs:

  - onTrue: runs the command once, at the moment you press. Like a doorbell: one
    press, one ring.
  - whileTrue: runs the command while you hold the button, and stops it when you
    let go. Like a flashlight button you have to keep squeezing.

  You've already seen both. X uses onTrue (one press locks the wheels), and A
  uses whileTrue (hold to face 0°).

  Your mission: make the B button count how many times it's been pressed, and
  log the count so we can watch it.

  You'll need three pieces in RobotContainer.java:
  1. A variable to hold the count.
  2. A binding on controller.b() that adds 1 to the count each time it's
     pressed.
  3. A Logger.recordOutput(...) that publishes the count, for example as
     Tutorial/ButtonPresses.

  First hint: look at how X is bound on line 122:

  controller.x().onTrue(Commands.runOnce(drive::stopWithX, drive));

  Commands.runOnce(...) runs some code one time. Question: if you wanted it to
  run your code (add 1 and log it) instead of drive::stopWithX, what would you
  put there? Take a shot. Rough code is fine, and I'll help fix it. I'll add the
  counter to the dashboard once your Java works.

  What I changed in RobotContainer.java:

    true when you press, and "end" logs false when you let go. This shows the
    difference between the two trigger types side by side.

  HELD light that turns green.

● That's half of it. ✅ Two things work together:

  1. onTrue only fires at the moment B changes from "not pressed" to "pressed."
     Holding it doesn't fire it again. It's a doorbell: holding the button still
     gives one ring.
  2. runOnce means that when the command does start, it runs its code one time
     and finishes right away.

  To get about 150, you'd need both changed: whileTrue(Commands.run(...)).
  Commands.run repeats every 20 ms for as long as the command stays scheduled,
  and whileTrue keeps it scheduled while you hold the button. (You can try it if
  you're curious. Just change it back afterward!)

  Phase 2: Your first mechanism, the intake roller 🌀

  The intake roller is the spinning part at the front of the robot that grabs 
  game pieces off the floor. It's the simplest mechanism there is: one motor
  spinning one roller. That makes it perfect for writing your first IO layer
  from scratch.

  ▎ Coming up:
  ▎ - Motors and motor controllers (C15): the motor is the muscle, and the 
  ▎ controller is the brain that tells it how hard to push.
  ▎ - Current limits (C16): a safety cap, so a stuck roller doesn't burn out the
  ▎ motor.
  ▎ - Open-loop control (C18): "give it 6 volts and hope," vs. measuring the 
  ▎ speed and correcting.
  ▎ - Rollers (C34): how intakes and indexers move game pieces.
  ▎
  ▎ Ask me about any of these whenever you're curious.

  Step 2: Design on paper first. You already know the outlet/plug idea from
  ModuleIO. Before we write any code, let's decide what our intake's "outlet"
  needs.

  Question: for a roller with one motor, what are 2 or 3 things the code would
  want to read from it (sensor values)? And what's the one thing it needs to
  tell it to do?

● Good thinking! ✅ Speed and spinning the motor are exactly right. Here's a
  closer look at the other two:

  - "Time running": the code can keep track of that itself with a timer, so the
    motor doesn't need to tell us. IO inputs are only things that come from the
    hardware.
  - "Limits": you're close! The real-world value is current: how many amps the
    motor is pulling. When a game piece jams the roller, the current spikes.
    That's how we'd notice a jam.

  So the standard roller "outlet" looks like this:

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
  │             Read (inputs)              │         Tell (outputs)         │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ connected: is the motor even talking   │ setVoltage(volts): spin this   │
  │ to us?                                 │ hard                           │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ velocityRadPerSec: how fast it's       │                                │
  │ spinning                               │                                │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ appliedVolts: how hard we're pushing   │                                │
  │ it                                     │                                │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ currentAmps: how much it's straining   │                                │
  └────────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

  ---

  Step 3 (continued): IntakeRollerIOSim, the fake-motor plug

  Back to my question: when we call setVoltage(6) on a simulated motor, the sim
  has to work out how fast that voltage would make a real motor spin. That
  depends on:
  - which motor it is (each motor model is stronger or faster than others)
  - the gear ratio (gearing trades speed for strength)
  - how heavy the roller is (its moment of inertia, meaning how hard it is to
    get spinning)

  WPILib's DCMotorSim does that physics for us. Each loop we give it the volts,
  tell it to step forward 20 ms, and read back the speed and current.

  🔍 Hardware detective moment. I checked the 2026 robot's intake roller on
  main:
  - It uses a TalonFX controller (CAN ID 20), 1:1 gearing, coast mode, and a 60
    A current limit.
  - But its sim model says DCMotor.getNEO(1). A NEO is a REV motor that can't
    run on a TalonFX! That's probably a copy-paste mistake.

  TalonFX controllers come built into Kraken X60 (and older Falcon 500) motors.
  Can you check with the mechanical team which motor is actually on the new 
  robot's intake roller? We need it for the sim model, and we never guess
  hardware facts. Until we know, we can start with Kraken X60 as a placeholder
  and mark it // TODO: verify.

  Want to try writing IntakeRollerIOSim yourself (ModuleIOSim.java is a good
  template), or should I walk you through it piece by piece?

  Where we are: Phase 2, building your first mechanism, the intake roller. Last
  time you designed and wrote IntakeRollerIO.java, the "outlet" that lists what

  current phase didn't change.

  ▎ Coming up:
  ▎ - Simulating a motor (C41): a physics model that pretends to be a real 
  ▎ motor, so the roller spins on a laptop.
  ▎ - Motors and motor controllers (C15): the motor is the muscle, and the 
  ▎ controller is the brain that tells it how hard to push.
  ▎ - Current limits (C16): a safety cap, so a jammed roller doesn't cook the 
  ▎ motor.
  ▎ - Open-loop control (C18): "give it 6 volts" vs. measuring the speed and 
  ▎ correcting.
  ▎
  ▎ Ask me about any of these whenever you're curious.

● Summary: We're writing IntakeRollerIOSim.java, a fake motor that lets the
  intake roller spin in simulation. It "plugs into" the IntakeRollerIO outlet
  you already wrote. It uses WPILib's DCMotorSim to work out how fast the roller
  would spin for a given voltage, and every 20 ms it reports the speed, volts,
  and current back to the robot code. We'll use ModuleIOSim.java as the
  template. Since we don't know the real motor yet, we'll use a Kraken X60 as a
  placeholder.

  In more detail:

  - Make the file. Create IntakeRollerIOSim.java in
    src/main/java/frc/robot/subsystems/intake/, next to IntakeRollerIO.java.
  - Plug it into the outlet. Declare it as public class IntakeRollerIOSim 
    implements IntakeRollerIO. implements is what makes it a "plug" that fits
    the interface.
  - Create the fake motor. Add a DCMotorSim field, built the same way as
    driveSim in ModuleIOSim (lines 32–36), with three values:
    - motor type: DCMotor.getKrakenX60(1) (placeholder, marked // TODO: verify 
      motor)
    - inertia: how hard the roller is to get spinning. A small number like 0.001
      works for a light roller.
    - gear ratio: 1.0, from the old robot's 1:1 gearing
  - Remember the voltage. Add a double appliedVolts field. setVoltage(volts)
    just saves the value there.
  - Run the physics in updateInputs. Every loop:
    a. Give the sim the volts, clamped to −12 to 12, because a battery can't
       give more than that.
    b. Step the sim forward 20 ms with update(0.02).
    c. Fill in the 4 inputs: connected = true, velocityRadPerSec and currentAmps
       from the sim, and appliedVolts from your field.
  - Use the template. ModuleIOSim does all of this for the drive motor. Copy its
    pattern, keep only one motor, and leave out the PID parts, since we're
    doing simple voltage control for now.
  - Test later. It won't do anything visible until we write the IntakeRoller
    subsystem and connect it in RobotContainer. That's the next step.

  Your first move: create the file and the DCMotorSim. What would you put for
  the three values? Or say "do it" and I'll write it with you, line by line.

● I created src/main/java/frc/robot/subsystems/intake/IntakeRollerIOSim.java,
  the fake-motor "plug" for your intake roller.

  What each part does:

  - implements IntakeRollerIO: makes this class a plug that fits your outlet, so
    it must provide updateInputs and setVoltage.
  - The 3 constants at the top:
    - GEARBOX = DCMotor.getKrakenX60(1): one Kraken X60 motor. It's a
      placeholder, marked TODO: verify.
    - GEAR_REDUCTION = 1.0: 1:1 gearing, from the old robot.
    - MOI_KG_METERS_SQ = 0.001: how hard the roller is to get spinning. It's a
      small number because the roller is light.
  - sim = new DCMotorSim(...): the physics model, built from those 3 values, the
    same way ModuleIOSim builds the drive motor.
  - setVoltage(volts): saves the voltage. That's all it does.
  - updateInputs(...), which runs every 20 ms:
    a. Feeds the volts into the sim, clamped to ±12 V like a real battery.
    b. Steps the physics forward 20 ms.
    c. Fills in the 4 inputs, so the rest of the code sees speed, volts, and
       current as if a real motor were there.

  How to test: save. The sim should restart with no build errors. Nothing moves
  yet, because no subsystem uses this file. That's next.

  ---

  Next step: the IntakeRoller subsystem. It's the "worker" that owns the roller.
  Each loop, it asks the IO for fresh inputs and logs them.

  Quick question: in RobotContainer, the drivetrain gets new ModuleIOSim() in
  sim mode and new ModuleIOKraken(...) on the real robot. What do you think the
  intake roller should get in sim mode?

● You made the change correctly. ✅ DCMotor.getFalcon500(1), and the comment
  says it's verified. Nice.

  But I have to correct myself: I told you the Falcon is "a little weaker and
  slower," and only the weaker part is true. I looked up the numbers WPILib
  uses:

  ┌────────────┬─────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │   Motor    │ Top speed (no load) │ Strength (stall torque) │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Falcon 500 │ 6380 RPM            │ 4.7 N·m                 │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Kraken X60 │ 6000 RPM            │ 7.1 N·m                 │
  └────────────┴─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  So your prediction was reasonable based on what I said, but the Falcon
  actually has a slightly higher top speed. What the Kraken has is a lot more
  strength (torque). That's the push it can give when something resists it, like
  a game piece jamming the roller.

  The lesson: "fast" and "strong" are two different things for a motor. Top
  speed is how fast it spins with nothing slowing it down. Torque is how hard it
  can push. This will matter when we pick current limits and look at how a game
  piece slows the roller down.

  And the best part is that we'll be able to check it on the dashboard once the
  roller is running. Real engineers trust measurements over guesses (even
  mentors' guesses 😅).

  RobotContainer, the drivetrain gets new ModuleIOSim() in sim mode. What should
  the intake roller get in sim mode?

● The answer: in sim mode, the intake roller gets new IntakeRollerIOSim(), the
  file we just made. It follows the same pattern as the drivetrain:

  ┌─────────────┬──────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Mode     │   Drivetrain gets…   │        Intake roller gets…         │
  ├─────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ REAL (on    │ new                  │ new IntakeRollerIOTalonFX() ← not  │
  │ the robot)  │ ModuleIOKraken(...)  │ written yet                        │
  ├─────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SIM         │ new ModuleIOSim()    │ new IntakeRollerIOSim() ✅         │
  │ (laptop)    │                      │                                    │
  ├─────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Replay      │ new ModuleIO() {}    │ new IntakeRollerIO() {} (an empty  │
  │             │                      │ plug that does nothing)            │
  └─────────────┴──────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  You pick the plug that matches where the code is running. That's the whole
  trick of IO layers.

  ---

  Next step: write IntakeRollerIOTalonFX, the real-hardware plug

  Summary: This is the plug that talks to the real Falcon 500 through its
  TalonFX controller, using CTRE's Phoenix 6 library. It does the same two jobs
  as the sim: setVoltage tells the motor how hard to push, and updateInputs
  reads speed, volts, and current back from it. We can't test it until the robot
  is on the carpet (Phase 3). Writing it now shows something important: this is
  the only file in the whole intake that knows what brand of motor we use. Our
  template is the drive-motor half of drive/ModuleIOKraken.java, which also uses
  a TalonFX.

  In more detail:

  - Make the file: IntakeRollerIOTalonFX.java in the intake/ folder, declared as
    implements IntakeRollerIO.
  - Create the motor: new TalonFX(canId). The 2026 robot used CAN ID 20 for the
    intake roller. (CAN IDs are like house numbers on the robot's wiring. We'll
    double-check this one on the real robot in Phase 3.)
  - Configure it with a TalonFXConfiguration (see ModuleIOKraken lines 114–127).
    Values from the 2026 robot:
    - Inverted: true, so "positive volts" spins the roller inward
    - Coast mode: when we stop pushing, the roller spins down freely instead of
      braking hard
    - Stator current limit: 60 A, a safety cap so a jammed roller doesn't
      overheat the motor (C16)
  - Status signals: Phoenix 6 gives you "signals" for velocity, applied volts,
    and current. In updateInputs, refresh them and copy them into the 4 inputs
    (see lines 185–186). connected is true when the refresh succeeds.
  - setVoltage: send a VoltageOut request to the motor (see line 72).
  - Constants: the CAN ID, inversion, and current limit are hardware facts, so
    they belong in one constants file, not scattered through the code (C45). The
    plan is a small IntakeConstants.java.

  Your first move: open ModuleIOKraken.java and find the lines that create and
  configure driveTalon (around lines 92–127). Which of those settings do you
  think our roller needs, and which are drive-only? Or say "do it" and I'll
  write it and walk you through it.

● Good instinct. ✅ The drive motor half is our template, because it's a TalonFX
  like our Falcon, and the turn half (SPARK MAX) gets dropped completely. But
  not every drive line fits a roller. Here's the sort:

  ✅ Keep (a roller needs these):
  - new TalonFX(canId): create the motor
  - TalonFXConfiguration with inversion and the stator current limit
  - PhoenixUtil.tryUntilOk(...) to apply the config (it retries if the CAN bus
    is busy)
  - status signals for velocity, applied volts, and current, plus refreshAll and
    the Debouncer for connected
  - VoltageOut for setVoltage

  🔄 Change:
  - NeutralModeValue.Brake → Coast. A drive wheel should stop hard, but a roller
    should spin down freely.

  ❌ Drop (drive-only):
  - the position signal and odometry frequency, because nobody needs to know how
    far the roller has rolled
  - setPosition(0.0), for the same reason
  - Slot0 kS/kV/kP/kD and VelocityVoltage: those are for closed-loop speed 
    control. We're doing simple voltage control for now (closed-loop comes in
    Phase 7).

  Quick check: why do you think a roller uses coast but a drive wheel uses
  brake? Think about what happens when the driver lets go of the stick, vs. when
  the intake stops.

  Answer that, and then say "do it" if you'd like me to write the file (plus a
  small IntakeConstants.java for the CAN ID and limits), or try it yourself.

● I wrote it. Three files changed in subsystems/intake/:

  1. IntakeConstants.java (new): every intake hardware fact in one place:
  - rollerGearbox = Falcon 500 and rollerMotorReduction = 1.0. These are
    verified, thanks to you.
  - rollerCanId = 20, rollerInverted = true, and rollerCurrentLimit = 60 A.
    These came from the 2026 robot, so they're marked TODO: verify in Phase 3.

  2. IntakeRollerIOTalonFX.java (new): the real-hardware plug. It's the
  drive-motor half of ModuleIOKraken, trimmed for a roller:
  - creates the TalonFX and configures coast mode, inversion, and the 60 A
    current limit
  - updateInputs: refreshes velocity, volts, and current from the motor.
    connected is true when the motor answers.
  - setVoltage: sends a VoltageOut request

  3. IntakeRollerIOSim.java (small change): now reads the motor and gear ratio
  from IntakeConstants instead of its own copies. Why? If the gearing ever
  changes, you change one number and both the sim and the real robot get it.
  Your "verified" comment moved with the Falcon line into the constants file.

  How to test: save. The sim should restart with no build errors. Nothing spins
  yet. That needs the subsystem, which is next.

  The answer to the coast/brake question: a drive wheel brakes so the robot
  stops where the driver lets go, instead of drifting into another robot. A
  roller coasts, because stopping it hard does nothing useful, and slamming it
  to a stop with a game piece half-inside puts stress on the motor and gears.

  ---

  Next step (step 5): the IntakeRoller subsystem, the "worker" that owns the
  roller. It's short:
  - it holds an IntakeRollerIO (it doesn't care which plug)
  - in periodic(), it calls io.updateInputs(inputs) and
    Logger.processInputs("IntakeRoller", inputs)
  - in RobotContainer, it gets created with the right plug for each mode (the
    table from before)

  Want to try writing periodic() yourself? It's two lines, and Drive.periodic()
  starts with the exact same pattern (lines 128–129). Or say "do it."

● All of it is in the src/main/java/frc/robot/subsystems/intake/ folder. Two

  - Lines 10–13: CAN ID, inversion, and current limit (marked TODO: verify)

  - Line 3: pulls in the constants from IntakeConstants
  - Line 21: creates the motor on CAN ID 20
  - Lines 23–33: the status signals (velocity, volts, current), the VoltageOut
    request, and the connected debouncer
  - Lines 35–53: the constructor that sets up the motor:
    - 36–44: the configuration: coast mode (line 38), inversion, gearing, and
      the 60 A current limit
    - 46–52: sets how often the motor reports its values
  - Lines 55–62: updateInputs, which reads the motor's values into the 4 inputs
  - Lines 64–67: setVoltage, which tells the motor how hard to push

  - Lines 11–16: the sim now uses rollerGearbox and rollerMotorReduction from
    IntakeConstants. Before, it had its own copies of the Falcon and 1:1 values.
    Your verified-motor comment moved to IntakeConstants.java line 6.
  - The rest of the file (updateInputs and setVoltage) didn't change.

  side). Click any file there and you'll see exactly what changed: new lines in
  green, removed lines in red.

  also provide the intake() and outtake() commands.

● Let's goooo! 🚀 It's ready to spin.

  What I added:

  IntakeRoller.java, lines 21–34: three commands:
  - intake(): pushes +6 V (spins the roller inward)
  - outtake(): pushes −6 V (spits pieces out)
  - stop(): pushes 0 V

  IntakeConstants.java, lines 15–18: the 6 V values. Why 6? The 2026 robot aimed
  for about 2900 RPM, and the math in the comment says that takes about 5.5 V.
  So 6 V is a good starting point to tune from.

  RobotContainer.java, in configureButtonBindings():
  - Default command = stop(): when nobody's pressing anything, the roller stops
    (C09)
  - Left trigger (whileTrue) → intake
  - Left bumper (whileTrue) → outtake

  Dashboard: a new Intake card with Volts, Roller RPM, and a light that shows
  STOPPED / INTAKING / OUTTAKING.

  ---

  🔮 Predict first: when you hold the left trigger, what will the card show for
  volts, and about what RPM? (Hint: the Falcon's top speed is about 6380 RPM at
  12 V, and you're giving it 6 V.)

  Then test it:
  1. Save, wait for the sim to restart, and enable Teleop.
  2. Hold the left trigger. The volts should show 6.0, the RPM should climb and
     settle, and the light should turn green: INTAKING.
  3. Let go. The roller should drop back to 0 and show STOPPED (the default
     command at work).
  4. Hold the left bumper. You should see negative volts, negative RPM, and
     OUTTAKING.

  Tell me what RPM you see. Was your prediction close? 🎯

● 🎉 IT SPINS! You just built your first mechanism from scratch: an IO
  interface, a sim plug, a real plug, a subsystem, commands, and button
  bindings. And it works on the dashboard. That's the whole AdvantageKit
  pattern, and every mechanism from here on follows it.

  Why 3209? The Falcon's top speed is 6380 RPM at 12 V. You gave it 6 V, which
  is half the voltage, and half of 6380 is about 3190. Very close to your 3209!
  In simple voltage control, speed goes up roughly in line with voltage: half
  the volts, about half the speed.

  ---

  Step 7: The catch with "just send volts" (C18) ⚡

  What we built is open-loop control: "push with 6 volts and hope." We never
  check whether the roller actually reached the speed we wanted. In sim it's
  perfect: 3209, every time. On a real robot, two things mess it up:

  1. The battery drains during a match. A full battery is about 12.5 V, a tired
     one might sag to 11 V or lower. "6 V" is still 6 V… but a heavy load pulls
     the battery down even further.
  2. A game piece gets squished into the roller. That's friction pushing back.

  Prediction question: say a game piece jams in the roller while you're holding
  intake at 6 V. What do you think happens to the RPM on the dashboard? And what
  happens to the current (amps)?

  (Commit reminder: your intake work isn't saved in git yet. Just say "commit" 
  whenever you want it saved.)

● Thanks for telling me, for real. I was talking like a textbook. 😅 I'll keep
  it simpler from here on. If something still doesn't make sense, just say

  Okay, let me redo that last part.

  ---

  Where we're at: your intake spins! 🎉 Hold the trigger and it goes about 3200
  RPM. That's huge.

  The thing I want you to think about:

  Right now our code basically says "push the roller with 6 volts." That's it.
  It never checks how fast the roller is actually going.

  It's like riding a bike and always pedaling with the same effort, no matter
  what:
  - On flat ground, you go a nice steady speed. 🚲
  - Going uphill? Same effort, but you slow way down.

  On the real robot, "uphill" happens when a game piece gets stuck in the roller
  and pushes back.

  So here's my question: if a game piece jams in the roller, what do you think
  happens to the roller's speed (RPM)? Go up, go down, or stay the same?

  Just a gut guess is totally fine. There's no wrong answer here, it's how we
  figure stuff out. 🙂

● Ooh, that's actually a really smart answer. You just came up with the fix! 🧠

  But here's the twist: our code doesn't do that yet. It's like a bike rider
  who's not paying attention. They keep pedaling with the same effort, so when
  they hit the hill (the stuck game piece), they just… slow down. The RPM drops,
  and our code doesn't even notice.

  What you described, "notice it's slowing down, so push harder," has a name:
  closed-loop control. The code keeps checking the speed and adjusts. It's like
  cruise control in a car: going uphill, the car gives it more gas by itself to
  keep the same speed. 🚗

  We'll build that later (Phase 7). For now, simple "just push 6 volts" is
  totally fine for an intake.

  Want me to commit it? Think of it like hitting "save" in a video game before
  the next level. 🎮

● Saved! 💾 Your intake is now locked in on your madtown branch:

  ▎ a67b702 Add intake roller with sim and TalonFX IO, commands, and dashboard 
  ▎ card

  If anything ever breaks later, we can always come back to this exact spot.

  ---

  One last quick thing before Phase 2 is done. It's just a "what do you think?"
  question, no code.

  Last year's robot didn't write its own intake code like we just did. It used a
  library called YAMS, which is basically a ready-made kit someone else built.
  You plug in a few numbers and it does the rest for you.

  This year, we wrote every piece ourselves.

  It's kind of like buying a pre-built PC vs. building your own:
  - Pre-built is faster to set up, but when something goes wrong, it's harder to
    figure out what's inside.
  - Building your own is more work, but you know exactly what every part does.

  What do you think? Can you think of one good thing about writing it ourselves,
  and one downside? Gut answers are totally fine!

  - IntakeRoller.java: the worker that gives the orders
  - IntakeRollerIO.java: the outlet, a list of what a roller can do
  - IntakeRollerIOTalonFX.java: the plug that actually talks to the Falcon
  - RobotContainer.java: where the buttons get hooked up

● 🎲 SIDE QUEST: The Panic Button 🚨

  The story: mid-match, your driver accidentally sucks up the wrong game piece.
  😱 They need to spit it out fast, but holding a button while also driving is
  hard. So they want this:

  ▎ Tap Y once → the intake spits for exactly 1 second, then stops by itself.

  Reward: you learn how to make commands that end on a timer. That's super
  useful later for autos! ⏱️

  ---

  You've got everything you need already. Here are your clues:

  🔍 Clue 1: you already have a command that spits: intakeRoller.outtake().

  🔍 Clue 2: remember the doorbell? onTrue = one tap starts it.

  🔍 Clue 3: any command can get a timer stuck on the end of it with
  .withTimeout(seconds). Like a microwave: it runs, and when the time's up,
  ding, it stops.

  Your mission: write one line in RobotContainer.java, right under the left
  bumper line, that hooks up Y.

  It'll look kind of like the left bumper line above it, but with the clues
  mixed in. Give it a shot, and when you think you've got it, say "check it."
  I'll look, and you can test it on the dashboard by watching the Intake light
  flash OUTTAKING for 1 second. 🎯

● Done! 🚨 Here's the line I added in RobotContainer.java, right under the left
  bumper:

  controller.y().onTrue(intakeRoller.outtake().withTimeout(1.0));

  Read it like a sentence:
  - controller.y().onTrue(...): "when Y gets tapped…"
  - intakeRoller.outtake(): "…start spitting…"
  - .withTimeout(1.0): "…and stop after 1 second." ⏱️

  And when it stops, the roller goes back to its default command (stop()) all by
  itself. That's the same "go back to the default job" idea from the drive
  code.

  Test it: save, wait for the sim to restart, and enable Teleop. Tap Y once and
  don't hold it. The Intake light should flash OUTTAKING for about 1 second,
  then go back to STOPPED.

  Bonus mini-challenge 🧩 (just think about it, no code): what do you think
  happens if you tap Y and then hold the left trigger (intake) before the 1
  second is up? Who wins, the panic button or the trigger?

  Try it in the sim and see if you guessed right!

✻ Worked for 18s · done 2:57 AM

❯ would making the time longer be better?                                       

● Great question! Honestly… it depends. It's a trade-off, kind of like how long
  to microwave popcorn. 🍿

  Longer (like 3 seconds):
  - ✅ More sure the bad piece is totally out
  - ❌ For 3 whole seconds, your intake is busy spitting instead of grabbing new
    pieces. In a 2-minute match, that adds up.

  Shorter (like 0.3 seconds):
  - ✅ Fast, and you're back to grabbing pieces quickly
  - ❌ Might not be long enough, so the piece only gets half spit out and gets
    stuck

  So how do real teams pick? They test it. On the real robot, you'd time how
  long it actually takes to spit a piece out, then add a tiny bit extra just to
  be safe. We can't know the perfect number from the laptop, because the sim
  doesn't have real game pieces.

  Wanna try it yourself? This one's just changing a number, so you can totally
  do it. 😄 In RobotContainer.java, find the 1.0 in the panic button line,
  change it to something like 3.0, and save. Then tap Y and watch how long
  OUTTAKING stays on.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)
● Love this idea. 🔦 And good news: VS Code can already do most of it, no new
  feature needed.

  What VS Code already does: whenever a line changes and hasn't been saved in
  git yet, VS Code puts a colored mark on it:
  - in the skinny strip on the right side of the code (the scrollbar)
  - in the thin bar just left of the line numbers

  🟩 Green = new lines, 🟦 blue = changed lines, 🔻 red triangle = deleted
  lines.

  So right now, if you open RobotContainer.java, you should see little marks in
  that right strip where the panic button line is. Click a mark and it jumps you
  right there. Try it! 👀

  Here's how I'll use it from now on:
  - When I change code: just look for the colored marks. That's everything I
    touched.
  - When it's YOUR turn to change something: I'll drop a comment right on the
    spot, like this:
  // 👉 YOUR TURN: change 1.0 to how many seconds you want
    That comment is a new line, so it shows up as a mark in the right strip too.
    Plus you can search for YOUR TURN (⌘F) and jump straight to it. When you're
    done, delete the comment.

  Want it even fancier? We could make YOUR TURN comments glow in a special
  color, like bright yellow. That means adding a feature to the team's VS Code
  buttons extension, though, and that's coach territory.

  Want to try the colored marks right now? Open RobotContainer.java and tell me
  if you see them on the right side!

● We haven't started Phase 3 yet. It's the next one! Here's what it's about, in
  plain words:

  Phase 3 = "Make it work on the REAL robot" 🤖

  Everything so far has been on the laptop. Phase 3 is when the code goes onto
  the actual robot and drives on carpet.

  What you'd do:
  1. Get the right apps on a laptop: the Driver Station (the app that turns the
     robot on and off) and the tools that talk to the motors.
  2. Tour the robot's insides with a coach: the "brain," the Wi-Fi radio, and
     how all the motors are wired together.
  3. Check our numbers: things like wheel size and motor ID numbers are still
     last year's robot. We make sure they match the new one.
  4. Put the code on the robot, with its wheels up on blocks first, so it can't
     run anyone over. 😅
  5. Fix each wheel so they all point straight and spin the right way.
  6. Measure how the robot really drives, so the code knows how hard to push the
     motors.
  7. Add a "reset which way is forward" button for the driver.
  8. Test your intake on the real robot, and finally check those TODO: verify
     numbers!

  The catch: most of these steps need a coach and the real robot, because real
  motors are strong and can hurt someone if something's set up wrong.

  When it's done: the robot drives smoothly on carpet, and the Field card on
  your dashboard shows it moving in real life. 🎉

  Do you have a coach and the robot around? If not, we can do step 3 (checking
  the numbers) on the laptop right now.

● Got it! We'll come back to Phase 3 once the robot's ready. I made a note so we
  don't forget to check those intake numbers.

  ---

  Phase 4: Vision 👀

  The big idea: teach the robot to see where it is on the field using cameras.

  Wait, doesn't it already know where it is? Kind of! Right now the robot
  figures out its position by counting how far its wheels have rolled. That's
  called odometry (fancy word, simple idea).

  It's like walking around your house with your eyes closed and counting your
  steps:
  - For a few seconds, you're pretty accurate. 👍
  - But every little slip adds up… and after a while you walk into a wall. 😵

  Robot wheels slip too, especially when another robot shoves yours. So the
  robot slowly gets more and more wrong about where it is.

  The fix: AprilTags! They're like QR codes stuck at known spots around the
  field. When the robot's camera sees one, it goes "oh, I know exactly where
  THAT tag is, so I must be right here." It's like opening your eyes for a
  second to check where you are. 👁️

  ---

  Quick question before we build anything: later, the turret is going to aim at
  the goal using the robot's position. What do you think happens to your shots
  if the robot thinks it's in the wrong spot? 🎯

● Exactly! 🎯 If the robot thinks it's 1 meter to the left of where it really
  is, the turret aims 1 meter off and misses every time. Bad position = bad aim.
  That's why vision comes first.

  ---

  Next step: give the robot "eyes" 📷

  Good news: we don't have to invent this from scratch. AdvantageKit (the
  toolkit we're already using) comes with a ready-made vision template, and last
  year's robot used it. And it uses the same plug system you already know:

  - VisionIO: the outlet ("what can a camera tell us?")
  - VisionIOPhotonVision: the plug for real cameras
  - VisionIOPhotonVisionSim: the plug for fake cameras in the sim. They "see"
    fake AprilTags on the fake field. 🤯
  - Vision: the worker that checks each camera's guess and passes the good ones
    to the drivetrain

  Here's my plan:
  1. Bring those 4 template files over from last year's robot. They're standard
     template code, the same for every team.
  2. Add a VisionConstants file with the camera names and where each camera is
     mounted.
  3. Hook it up in RobotContainer so the drivetrain uses what the cameras see.

  One problem: I don't know how many cameras the new robot has, or where they're
  mounted. Last year's robot had 4 (two in front, two in back). For now I can
  use last year's setup as a placeholder marked TODO: measure on new robot, so
  the sim works today. We'd fix the real numbers when the robot's ready.

  Sound good? Say yes and I'll set it up. Or, if you know the new robot's
  cameras, tell me!

● Oh, this is a sick idea. 🔥 I built it on the Field card, since that's where
  the robot drives around, so you can see exactly where each camera is looking.

  What you'll see:
  - 4 cones coming out of the robot, one per camera. Each shows what that camera
    can see: how wide it looks and how far (about 25 feet, the farthest we
    trust).
  - Cone colors:
    - 🟢 Green: the camera sees tags and its guess got accepted
    - 🔴 Red: it sees tags, but its guess got rejected
    - ⚪ Faint gray: it doesn't see any tags right now
  - Rays (your "raycast"): a line from the camera to each tag it's looking at,
    with a dot on the tag. 🎯

  The cones spin with the robot, so when you turn, you'll see them sweep across
  the field.

  Heads up: I copied where the cameras sit on the robot into the dashboard code.
  If someone ever moves a camera, they'd need to update it in two places:
  VisionConstants.java and the dashboard. I left a comment so nobody forgets.

  ---

● Ooh, so you want the sim to feel like a real field, where you can't drive
  through walls. 🧱 This one's bigger, so here's my plan first. Tell me if it
  sounds good.

  Part 1: Draw the field stuff (dashboard)
  - A thick border around the field
  - The two goals (hubs) as squares, colored blue and red for each alliance

  Part 2: "Wall physics" (robot code, sim only)

  Right now the sim robot is a ghost. 👻 It drives right through everything,
  because the sim only knows how the wheels spin, not what's around them. My
  plan:

  - Make a new file, FieldBoundaries.java, that knows where the walls and goals
    are.
  - Every loop (50 times a second), it checks: "is the robot poking through a 
    wall or into a goal?" If yes, it pushes the robot back out to the edge.
  - Where do the goal sizes come from? I won't guess. I'll work them out from
    the AprilTags stuck on each goal's sides, since the official field map says
    exactly where those tags are.
  - It only runs in the sim, never on the real robot (the real robot has real
    walls 😄).
  - The robot code also sends the goal shapes to the dashboard, so the drawing
    and the physics always match, from one source.

  Honest heads-up about the limits: it's not real physics. The robot won't
  bounce or spin when it hits something. It just stops at the wall, like hitting
  an invisible barrier. The wheels keep spinning, just like a real robot
  pushing against a wall.

  Files I'd touch: a new FieldBoundaries.java, a few lines in
  RobotContainer.java and Robot.java to run it every loop, and the dashboard.

  Sound good? Say yes and I'll build it! 🛠️

● The walls are in! 🧱 Here's what changed.

  On the dashboard (Field card):
  - A thick border around the field. Those are the walls.
  - Two HUB squares, one blue and one red.

  The "wall physics" (new file: sim/FieldBoundaries.java):
  - 50 times a second it checks: is the robot poking through a wall or into a 
    hub? If so, it pushes the robot back to the edge.
  - It knows the robot is a 28-inch square, so it even accounts for the robot
    being turned sideways (a turned square sticks out farther at the corners).
  - The hub sizes come from the AprilTags on the hubs in the official field map,
    not from a guess.
  - It only runs in the sim. Robot.java calls it from simulationPeriodic(),
    which never runs on the real robot.

  🕵️ Bonus bug I found: the dashboard was drawing the field 17.5 m long, which
  looks like last year's field. This year's is about 16.5 m. So the robot
  would've hit an invisible wall a meter before the drawn one! Now the robot
  code tells the dashboard the real field size, so the drawing and the walls
  always match.

  ---

  Test it:
  1. Save, wait for the sim to restart, and enable Teleop.
  2. Drive full speed into a wall. The robot should stop right at the border. 💥
  3. Drive into a HUB. It should stop at the edge of the square and slide along
     it if you push at an angle.
  4. Spin while touching a wall. The robot gets nudged out a bit as its corners
     swing around.

  One thing to check for me 👀: do the HUB squares look like they're in the
  right spots and about the right size? I built them from the tag positions, and
  if the tags aren't on all 4 sides of the hub, the square might come out a bit
  too small. Tell me what you see!

● Good catch! 🕵️ You're right, the cameras had X-ray vision. They could see tags
  straight through the hubs, which a real camera can't do. Fixed!

  How it works now:
  - 50 times a second, for each camera, the code draws an imaginary straight
    line from the camera to every tag on the field.
  - If that line goes through a hub, the tag is hidden, and that camera doesn't
    get to see it this time. 🚫
  - If the line is clear, the camera can see it (as long as the tag is also in
    front of the camera and close enough, like before).

  One tricky part I had to handle: the tags on a hub are stuck right on its 
  sides. A tag on the side facing you shouldn't get hidden by the side it's
  stuck to. So for this check, the hub gets treated as a tiny bit smaller (5 cm
  on each side). Tags on the near side are visible, and tags on the far side are
  hidden. ✅

  Where the code lives:
  - FieldBoundaries.java: new isBlocked(...) method that answers "is a hub in
    the way?" It uses the same hub squares as the walls, so it's one source of
    truth again.
  - VisionIOPhotonVisionSim.java: before each camera "looks," it only gets the
    tags that aren't blocked.

  Limits (so you're not surprised):
  - Only the hubs block the view. Other robots and other field stuff don't, yet.
  - It's top-down only, so it ignores height. A camera can't peek over a hub in
    this sim.

  ---

  Test it: save and wait for the sim to restart.
  1. Drive so a hub is between the robot and some tags on the far side.
  2. Watch the rays on the Field card. No ray should pass through a hub. ✅
  3. Drive around the hub. Rays to the far tags should pop in once you have a
     clear view.

  Does it look right? 👀

● Back on track! 🛤️ We're still in Phase 4: Vision. Here's where you are:

  - ✅ Why vision matters (eyes closed vs. eyes open)
  - ✅ Gave the robot cameras, plus all your bonus stuff: camera cones, walls,
    hubs, no X-ray vision, the intake block
  - ⏭️ Measuring the real cameras: skipped for now, since we need the robot
  - 👉 Next: how the robot decides which camera guesses to TRUST

  But first, save point! 💾 You've built a ton since the last save (vision,
  walls, camera cones). Want me to commit it?

  ---

  The idea for this step: not every camera guess is a good guess 🤔

  Think about asking people for directions:
  - Someone standing right next to a street sign: you trust them a lot. 👍
  - Someone squinting at a sign far away: eh, kind of trust them.
  - Someone who says you're standing on the roof: nope, ignore them. 🙃

  The robot does the same thing with camera guesses. In Vision.java, it does two
  things:
  1. Throws out guesses that are obviously wrong. That's the REJECTED you've
     been seeing.
  2. For the rest, decides how much to trust each one. Close tags get trusted
     more, far tags less.

  Your mission (just reading, no coding): open Vision.java and search (⌘F) for
  rejectPose. You'll find a list of reasons a guess gets thrown out. There are
  comments next to most of them.

  Question: pick one of those reasons and tell me, in your own words, why it
  makes sense to throw that guess out.

❯ why dod we want to amke the distance smaller?                                 

● Great question! Honestly, we don't necessarily want to. It's an experiment, so
  you can see what this number does. 🧪

  It's a trade-off, kind of like deciding who to ask for directions:

  Smaller distance (like 15 ft): picky 🧐
  - ✅ Only trusts close tags, which give really accurate guesses
  - ❌ Ignores more tags, so the robot goes longer without a "check where I am."
    In between, it's back to counting steps with its eyes closed, and the drift
    builds up.

  Bigger distance (like 40 ft): trusts everyone 🤷
  - ✅ Gets lots of updates, from almost anywhere on the field
  - ❌ Some updates come from tiny, far-away tags, so the robot's position might
    jump around a bit

  So what's the right number? It depends on how good your real cameras are. You
  said yours aren't great, so a smaller number might actually be smart! But the
  only way to know for sure is to test on the real robot: drive around, watch
  whether the position stays steady or jumps, and adjust.

  Changing it to 15 in the sim right now just lets you see the trade-off:
  shorter cones, more yellow "too far" rays, more REJECTED. Then you can change
  it back to 25 (or keep it, your call!). 😄

✻ Churned for 1m 3s · done 3:42 AM

❯ when we get close to the hub the camera is rejecting those tags why?          

● Great observation! 🕵️ Let's figure it out like engineers. Here are the reasons
  Vision.java throws out a guess:

  1. No tags in the guess
  2. Only 1 tag, and it's "ambiguous" (more on this below)
  3. The guess says the robot is floating in the air or underground
  4. The tag is too far (your 20 ft rule)
  5. The guess puts the robot outside the field

  Close to the hub, #3, #4, and #5 probably aren't it. My top suspect is #2. 🔎

  Why #2? The hub has tags on its sides, usually a couple next to each other.
  Far away, a camera sees 2+ tags at once, and multiple tags make a solid guess.
  But when you get really close, the camera's view is narrower, so it might
  only fit 1 tag in the picture.

  With only one tag, the math can get confused. A flat square can look almost
  the same from two different angles, kind of like how a coin looks like an oval
  from both the left and the right. That confusion is called ambiguity. Our
  code says: "only 1 tag and more than 10% confused? Reject it." That's a pretty
  strict rule.

  But I'm guessing, and good engineers don't guess. They check! 📊

● Done! 🎥 The camera views now show a 3D field behind the tags.

  What you'll see in each camera view:
  - a floor grid (one line every meter), so you can tell how far away things are
  - the field edges as bright white lines, plus the center line
  - the hubs as tall 3D boxes (72 inches high), blue and red
  - the tags and their highlight boxes from the camera, right on top, in the
    same spots

  How it works: PhotonVision's own field drawing wasn't showing up, so I built
  our own. For each camera, the dashboard does the same 3D math the camera does:
  it takes where the camera is on the robot, how it's tilted, and where the
  robot is on the field, and works out where every line should land in the
  picture. Then it draws the field behind the camera video and makes the video's
  black background see-through. The two layers line up. 🎯

  🤔 You might notice the views look UPSIDE DOWN! That's not a bug. Last year's
  cameras were mounted upside down on the robot, and the code says so. So the
  views look exactly like what the real cameras would see. In fact, last year's
  team had a lesson about this: "all cameras are flipped on this robot." Real
  teams fix it with a "flip image" setting in PhotonVision. Now you know why!

  Small things you might notice:
  - While driving fast, the field lines might lag a tiny bit behind the tags,
    because the video takes a moment to arrive. When you stop, they line up.
  - I copied the camera heights and tilts into the dashboard code, so if a
    camera moves, update it in both places (there's a comment).

  ---

  Test it: the dashboard reloads by itself.
  1. Look at the Camera Views card. You should see the grid and field edges now,
     not just black.
  2. Drive toward a hub and watch the blue or red box get bigger.
  3. Check that the tags sit right on the hub's sides in the picture.

  Does it line up? 👀 And don't forget your detective mission: what does the
  REJECTED reason say near the hub? 🔍

● Done! 🎨 Now in the Camera Views, every tag a camera sees gets a glowing 
  outline:

  - 🩵 Cyan: the tag's guess was used for the robot's position
  - 💜 Magenta (pink-purple): the camera sees the tag, but the guess got
    rejected

  Why cyan and magenta? PhotonVision already draws its own green and red boxes
  around tags. Using different colors means you can always tell our highlight
  apart from PhotonVision's boxes. Nice thinking to ask for that!

  Other details:
  - The outlines are drawn a little bigger than the tags, so they frame the tag
    instead of covering it up.
  - They sit on top of the video, so they stay bright and easy to see.
  - They use the same 3D camera math as the field lines, so they land right on
    the tags, and they flip along with the picture.
  - There's a small legend under the camera views showing which color means
    what.

  Under the hood: the field drawing and the tag outlines both needed "where does
  this 3D point land in the picture?" So I moved that math into one shared 
  helper (cameraView) that both use, instead of having two copies. 👍

  ---

  1. Drive toward some tags. They should get cyan outlines.
  2. Drive up close to the hub, where you were seeing rejections. Those tags
     should turn magenta.
  3. Compare with the Vision card. The camera showing REJECTED should be the one
     with magenta tags.

  And now that you can see the rejected tags… what does the reason say on the
  Vision card? 🔍

● Good call reporting it. That was a real bug, and it was mine. Fixed! 🔧

  What went wrong: when I added the cyan and magenta outlines, I put one line of
  code in the wrong order. It used a helper before the line that created it. In
  JavaScript, that's like calling a friend before you've saved their number: it
  just fails. The camera card crashed right away, so it never looked for the
  camera videos and sat on "Waiting…" forever.

  The fix: I swapped the two lines into the right order.

  How I checked it this time: I made a test that runs the whole dashboard with a
  fake browser and fake robot data (fake cameras, fake tags, a fake rejected
  pose), so I can catch crashes without your laptop. Then I put the bug back
  into a copy, and the test caught it: Cannot access 'scheduleScenes' before 
  initialization. So the test really works, and the fixed version passes. ✅

● I added a Copy NetworkTables card to the dashboard. 📋

  How to use it:
  1. (Optional) Type a word in the box to copy only matching values. For
     example, Vision copies just the camera stuff, and IntakeRoller copies just
     the intake. Leave it empty to copy everything. That can be a lot!
  2. Click Copy. It should say "Copied 42 values! Now paste them to Claude."
  3. Paste it here (⌘V).

  What the pasted text looks like:
  NetworkTables snapshot (8:59:53 PM), 10 values
  /AdvantageKit/RealOutputs/Field/Size = [16.5410, 8.0520]
  /AdvantageKit/RealOutputs/Odometry/Robot = struct 24 bytes: [3, 4, 0]
  /AdvantageKit/RealOutputs/Vision/Camera0/LastResult = "REJECTED: height off (z
  = 0.91 m)"
  Positions and other "packed" values come through as their actual numbers. For
  example, Odometry/Robot = [3, 4, 0] means x = 3 m, y = 4 m, facing 0 radians.
  That makes them easy for me to read.

  I tested it with fake robot data before handing it to you, so it should work
  on the first try. 🤞

  Perfect first use: drive up to the hub until a camera says REJECTED, type
  Vision in the box, click Copy, and paste it here. Then I can see exactly
  what's going on and we can finally solve the hub mystery! 🔍

✻ Cogitated for 51s · done 4:00 AM

❯ seems like once the camera sees poses that are too far away, ALL of the tags  
  are rejected for that camera                                                  

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● You figured it out! 🎉 You solved the hub mystery. I checked the code, and
  you're exactly right. Here's why:

  When a camera sees several tags at once, it combines them into one guess,
  which is usually more accurate. Then it checks that combined guess against the
  max distance using the average distance of all the tags it saw.

  It's like your grade average: 🧮
  - Close hub tags: 2 m, 2 m, 2 m (great grades)
  - One tag way across the field: 12 m (one terrible grade)
  - Average: (2 + 2 + 2 + 12) ÷ 4 = 4.5 m

  Near the hub, though, it's usually worse, because the far tags pile up. One or
  two far ones can drag the average past your 20 ft (about 6 m) limit, and then
  the whole guess gets thrown out, even the good close tags. 😩 That's exactly
  what you saw: one far tag ruins it for everyone.

  ---

  How should we fix it? Here are two ideas. Which one do you like?

  🅰️ Check the closest tag instead of the average
  "If at least one tag is close, trust the guess."
  - ✅ Super simple, basically a one-line change
  - ❌ The far tags are still mixed into the combined guess, which adds a little
    error. But the trust math already gives far tags less weight, so it's not
    terrible.

  🅱️ Backup plan: if the combined guess is rejected, fall back to the closest 
  single tag
  "If the team guess fails, ask the most reliable person alone."
  - ✅ Only uses close, trustworthy tags when there's a problem
  - ❌ More code. Also, single-tag guesses can be "ambiguous," the flat-square
    confusion from earlier.

  There's no perfect answer, it's a trade-off. What do you think: A or B? And
  why? 🤔

✻ Cooked for 24s · done 4:01 AM

❯ can we just reject specific far tags?                                         

● Yes, and that's the cleanest idea of all! 🧠 Throw out only the far tags, then
  make the combined guess from the close ones that are left.

  The catch: the camera computer (PhotonVision) does the "combine the tags" math
  by itself, using all the tags it sees. So to leave the far ones out, our code
  has to redo that math itself with just the close tags. PhotonVision has a
  helper for exactly that. Next I'm checking the exact version we use so I call
  it the right way.

  is blocked, so I'll implement the method following how recent versions of
  PhotonVision's pose estimator do it. If the build throws an error, send it to
  me and I'll fix it.

❯ is this better solved in photonvision config?                                 

● Good question to stop and ask! And to be clear: I haven't changed any code 
  yet. I was only reading the file when you stopped me.

  Short answer: maybe, but I'm not sure PhotonVision has that setting. Here's
  what I know and what I don't.

  What I'm not sure about: I don't know for certain whether the PhotonVision
  version you use has a "ignore tags smaller than X" (or farther than X) filter
  for AprilTags. Far tags look small, so a "minimum tag size" filter would do
  the same job. Someone needs to open the PhotonVision web page on the camera
  computer and look at the AprilTag pipeline settings. I'd rather you check than
  have me guess and be wrong.

  If PhotonVision does have it, here's the trade-off:

  ┌────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────┐
  │            │   Fix it in PhotonVision settings    │   Fix it in our    │
  │            │                                      │     robot code     │
  ├────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Effort     │ ✅ Just a setting, no code           │ ❌ More code to    │
  │            │                                      │ write and maintain │
  ├────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Speed      │ ✅ The camera computer does the work │ ❌ The roboRIO     │
  │            │                                      │ redoes the math    │
  ├────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │            │ ❌ On the camera computer, not in    │ ✅ In git, so      │
  │ Where it   │ git. If someone resets or swaps it,  │ everyone can see   │
  │ lives      │ the setting is gone, and nobody can  │ it and undo it     │
  │            │ see the history.                     │                    │
  ├────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Works in   │ ❌ Not automatically. We'd have to   │ ✅ Same code runs  │
  │ the sim?   │ copy the setting into the sim        │ in sim and on the  │
  │            │ separately.                          │ robot              │
  ├────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ One place  │ ❌ The distance lives in two places  │ ✅ One number:     │
  │ for the    │ (camera settings + maxDistance)      │ maxDistance        │
  │ number?    │                                      │                    │
  └────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────┘

  My recommendation: check PhotonVision first, because if the setting exists,
  it's the simpler fix on the real robot. If you go that way, also:
  - Write the setting down (or export the PhotonVision settings file into the
    repo), so it doesn't get lost.
  - I'd make the sim match it, using a sim setting for how far the fake cameras
    can see, so the sim still behaves like the real robot.

  If PhotonVision doesn't have it, we go back to the robot-code fix.

  Do you have access to the camera computer, or a coach who does? If not, we can
  do the robot-code version for now and move it to PhotonVision later.

  ---

  Step 2: Draw your first path! 🖊️

  Here's the goal: at the start of a match, drive the robot out of the starting 
  zone. It's the simplest auto there is, and it scores points in most games.

  In PathPlanner:
  1. Click Paths on the left. You'll see an "Example Path" that came with the
     template. Leave it alone and click + (Add Path) to make a new one.
  2. Name it something like Drive Out.
  3. You'll see a path with two dots:
     - The start dot is where the robot begins. Drag it near your alliance wall
       (the blue side, on the left).
     - The end dot is where it finishes. Drag it a few meters out toward the 
       middle, but stop before the hub!
  4. Drag the little handles on each dot to curve the path if you want.
  5. PathPlanner saves automatically.

  🔮 Predict: when the robot follows this path, what do you think happens if the
  robot's starting position in the sim doesn't match where the path starts? 🤔

  Tell me when your path is drawn!

● Whoa, you went way past "drive out of the zone"! 🔥 I looked at your files:
  - Path "!": from near your wall out toward the middle
  - Path "2": a whole loop with 5 points that comes back toward the wall
  - "New Auto": runs "!" and then "2", back to back

  That's already what real autos look like: small paths chained together. 👏

  And it answers my prediction question! Your auto has "Reset Odometry" turned
  on. That means when the auto starts, the robot is told "you're at the start of
  path '!' now." Without it, if the robot were somewhere else, it would try to
  drive from the wrong spot. It might cut across the field to catch up, or crash
  into things! 😬

  Small tip: names like ! and 2 are hard to understand later. Try renaming them
  in PathPlanner to things like Start To Middle and Loop Back, and the auto to
  Drive Out Loop. Future-you (and your teammates) will thank you.

  ---

  Step 3: Run it! 🚀

  The dashboard didn't have a way to pick an auto, so I added an Auto card:
  - a dropdown listing every auto you make in PathPlanner
  - "Robot will run": which auto the robot actually has selected, so you can
    confirm it heard you
  - an auto timer that counts up while auto runs. A real auto only gets 20 
    seconds! ⏱️

  Try it:
  1. Save in PathPlanner, then let the sim restart. The robot only reads paths
     when it starts up.
  2. On the Auto card, pick your auto from the dropdown. "Robot will run" should
     change to match.

  4. Hands off the controller! 🙌 Watch the robot drive your path on the Field
     card.

● Good instinct to suspect the timers! They're actually tiny jobs, though. The
  real cause was too much drawing. 🎨💥

  What was happening:
  - Every time the robot moved (up to 60 times a second), the dashboard redrew 8
    big pictures for the camera views (4 field drawings + 4 tag outline
    layers), each 960×720 pixels. That's like repainting 8 posters 60 times a
    second!
  - On top of that, the sim was drawing its own field lines into all 4 camera
    videos, which slowed down the robot code too.

  What I fixed:
  1. Camera drawings are half size now. You won't notice in those small boxes,
     but it's 4× less work. 📉
  2. The camera drawings update 15 times a second instead of 60. Still smooth
     for watching, and 4× less work again.
  3. Turned off the sim's own field lines. We draw the field ourselves now, so
     the sim doesn't need to. That frees up the robot code too.

  The Field card still updates at full speed, so driving should feel smooth.

  I re-ran my crash test with fake robot data, and everything still works. ✅

  ---

  Test it: save so the sim restarts, then refresh the dashboard once.
  - Drive around. Does it feel smoother now?
  - Still janky? Open VisionConstants.java and set simCameraStreams = false.
    That turns off the 4 videos, which are the heaviest part. Everything else,
    including the cone rays on the Field card, keeps working.

  Tell me how it feels! Then let's go run your auto. 🚀

● Confirmed! 🎯 19 restarts in one run means the robot keeps getting disabled 
  and re-enabled, and each re-enable starts your auto over. Mystery solved. Nice
  testing!

  Next question: WHO is too busy? The heartbeat can get lost in two places:
  - the dashboard freezes while drawing, so it stops sending "I'm here!" 💓
  - the sim is so busy it can't keep up with messages

  Both of those get a lot busier because of the 4 camera videos: the sim has to
  make them, and the dashboard has to show them. So they're suspect #1.

  Let's test it, changing just one thing at a time. That's how real engineers
  debug: if you change 3 things at once and it gets fixed, you don't know which
  one did it. 🧪

  🎮 YOUR TURN:
  1. Open VisionConstants.java.
  2. Find the colored mark on the right (or search ⌘F for YOUR TURN).
  3. Change simCameraStreams = true to false and save. The sim restarts.
  4. Refresh the dashboard, then run New Auto again.
  5. Watch Auto started.

  What it means:
  - Stays at 1 time and the path runs all the way through: the camera videos
    were the problem! ✅ Then we decide how to make them lighter, or just keep
    them off while running autos.
  - Still restarting: something else is too busy, and we keep hunting. 🔍

  Tell me the number!

✻ Worked for 21s · done 5:33 AM

※ recap: You're getting your first autonomous path running in sim, but the auto keeps restarting because the robot briefly disables. Next, set simCameraStreams to false in VisionConstants.java, rerun New Auto, and check whether Auto started stays at 1.

❯ a coache fixed it for me                                                      

  Ran 4 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Awesome, shoutout to your coach! 🙌 I looked at the fix to see what the real
  problem was, and I'll be honest: my guess was wrong.

  What was actually happening: there were two dashboards open at the same time
  (maybe two browser tabs, or two windows). Both were talking to the sim:
  - Your dashboard said: "Enabled! Run auto!" 🟢
  - The other one, sitting idle, kept saying: "Disabled!" 🔴

  The robot listened to both and kept flip-flopping. Every flip back to enabled
  restarted your auto. That's your 19 restarts!

  The coach's fix: now only one dashboard is "the driver" at a time. Whichever
  one you click last takes control, and the others go quiet. If another
  dashboard has control, you'll see a button saying so, which you can click to
  take over.

  The lesson 🧠: our counter test was still super useful, because it proved what
  was happening (the robot kept getting disabled). But my guess about why was
  wrong. Engineers get that part wrong all the time. That's why you keep testing
  instead of trusting the first idea, even when it sounds smart. 😅

  ---

  Back on track! Phase 5's finish line: your auto needs to work 3 times in a row
  in the sim. 🏁

  1. Pick New Auto on the Auto card.
  2. Switch to Autonomous → Enable, and watch it drive the whole path.
  3. Disable, then do it 2 more times.

  Check that "Auto started" goes up by just 1 each run, and that the robot
  finishes the whole loop. Tell me how it goes, and what the auto timer says at
  the end! ⏱️
